### F-EW-088 — "I'll ask Pauline's permission" — and tears when refused
- Claim: After her mother's death, Thérèse asked permission for everything. When her father invited her out, she always said she would ask Pauline. Her father encouraged this. If Pauline refused, Thérèse sometimes cried, because her father would have liked her company, but she obeyed without insisting.
- When / where: 1877–1882, Les Buissonnets, Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Pauline; Louis Martin
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW
- Source: PO 1, f. 140v, Session 5, 12 Aug 1910
- New to English readers: partial (HA mentions the permission; the refusals and tears are added)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: A-F28
### F-EW-089 — Alms at the door of Les Buissonnets on Mondays
- Claim: Every Monday the poor came to Les Buissonnets. At each ring Thérèse opened the door and came to describe them to Pauline (a lame old man; a pale woman with tiny children, one in swaddling clothes). Then she ran to bring bread or money. Her father gave her coins as a reward for her work, and she spent them all on alms.
- When / where: 1877–1882, Les Buissonnets, Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Pauline; Louis Martin; the poor
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW and HEARD
- Source: PO 1, f. 212v, Session 14, 27 Aug 1910
- Original words: « Pauline, le pauvre m'a dit: Le bon Dieu vous bénira, ma petite demoiselle. »
- Translation: "Pauline, the poor man told me: God will bless you, my little miss."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — eyewitness
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: A-F45
### F-EW-090 — Mother firm, father gentle; the mother wanted all her daughters to be nuns
- Claim: Their mother was rather firm in education and let nothing pass, especially vanity. She had them lift their hearts to God during the day and took them on visits to the Blessed Sacrament. She wanted all her daughters to be nuns but did not want to influence them. The father was gentler and especially loved Thérèse.
- When / where: 1860s–1877, Alençon
- People: Zélie Martin; Louis Martin; the daughters
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW
- Source: PO 1, f. 139v–140r, Session 5, 12 Aug 1910
- Original words: « Tu la perdras! »
- Translation: "You'll spoil her!" (the mother to the father about Thérèse)
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: A-F149
### F-EW-091 — Why the family moved to Lisieux; who raised Thérèse
- Claim: After Zélie's death (28 Aug 1877) Louis moved to Lisieux because Isidore Guérin, Zélie's brother, lived there; he hoped Mme Guérin, "particularly kind and pious", would help raise his daughters. Thérèse was brought up at home by her father, Marie and Pauline until 8½, then became a half-boarder at the Benedictine Abbey, taking the place of Léonie, who had finished; Céline was there with her.
- When / where: 1877–1881, Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin; Isidore and Céline Guérin; Marie; Pauline; Léonie; Céline; Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW
- Source: PO 1, f. 140v–141r, Session 5, 12 Aug 1910
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The elder girls went to the Visitation of Le Mans; the younger ones to the Lisieux Benedictines.
- Extraction ID: A-F150
### F-EW-092 — Pauline was Thérèse's teacher before the Abbey
- Claim: In the years before the Abbey school, Pauline was especially in charge of Thérèse's education. Thérèse was very diligent, profited from every lesson, and already took the habit of never complaining or excusing herself. At the Abbey weekly reports sent home showed her full diligence.
- When / where: 1877–1881, Les Buissonnets
- People: Pauline; Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès; her own role

### F-EW-093 — Father closed his jewellery shop on Sundays despite lost sales
- Claim: Louis Martin closed his jewellery shop on Sundays. Friends called this excessive, because country people came to town on Sundays to buy wedding jewellery. They urged him to leave just a side door open. He answered that he preferred God's blessing.
- When / where: Alençon, before 1877
- People: Louis Martin; his friends
- Witness: Mère Agnès, saw and heard as a child
- Source: PA 6, [344], S11 5 Jul 1915

### F-EW-094 — Mother's dream: all daughters Carmelites, and her own plan for the Visitation
- Claim: Zélie was struck by the life of Madame Acarie. Pauline often heard her say that it would be a great honour to have all one's daughters Carmelites. Zélie told Pauline that if Louis died first, she would end her days in a Visitation monastery once all the children were settled.
- When / where: Alençon, before 1877
- People: Zélie Martin; Pauline
- Witness: Mère Agnès, HEARD her mother

### F-EW-095 — After her mother's death: shy, cried easily, mostly from fear of giving pain
- Claim: After her mother died, Thérèse became very shy with strangers and so sensitive that a trifle made her cry. Pauline noticed that the cause of the tears was usually fear of having hurt her father, her sisters, or above all God.
- When / where: 1877–1886, Lisieux (Les Buissonnets)
- People: Thérèse; Pauline
- Witness: Mère Agnès, SAW

### F-EW-098 — Childhood obedience: "I'll ask Pauline"; sent alone into the dark garden
- Claim: As a child she never disobeyed Pauline; Pauline's advice was an order. When her father invited her out after lessons, she always said she would ask Pauline's leave; he encouraged this. To cure her fear of the dark, Pauline sent her alone at night anywhere in the house and even the garden; she obeyed and finally overcame her fear. She shut her book the moment reading time ended. One book had a picture Pauline forbade her to look at; if the book fell open there, she closed it quickly.
- When / where: 1877–1882, Les Buissonnets
- People: Thérèse; Pauline; Louis Martin
- Witness: Mère Agnès, SAW; she gave the orders

### F-EW-101 — "Practices" counted on a homemade string of beads
- Claim: At four, Thérèse counted her small acts of virtue and sacrifices on a kind of string of beads made for the purpose. She called them "pratiques". In her games with her sisters "practices" came up all the time, which puzzled a neighbour. Her practices were mostly giving way to her sister, which cost her effort, because she was then very headstrong.
- When / where: c. 1877, Alençon/Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Céline; a neighbour (unnamed)

### F-EW-102 — After 1877: excessively timid, despised by other girls
- Claim: After Zélie's death her happy character changed. She was cheerful only with the family at the Buissonnets; everywhere else she was excessively shy. Other little girls found her clumsy at their games and looked down on her, though she joined in with good grace. She suffered from their unkind ways and began to like hiding and not being seen, sincerely believing herself inferior.

- People: Thérèse; unnamed girls
- Witness: Céline — SAW
- Source: PO 4, f. 336v, S27

### F-EW-103 — Opposition: Uncle Guérin thought her education incomplete; society thought her incapable
- Claim: Opposition: From 1877 to Christmas 1886 Thérèse went through "a period of darkening". Her teachers saw her intelligence, but in society she passed for incapable and clumsy, because her extreme shyness paralysed her. Uncle Isidore Guérin said her instruction had been cut short and her education left incomplete. She said almost nothing and always let others talk.

### F-EW-104 — Near-constant headaches; she cried for having cried
- Claim: In childhood she suffered in body as well as heart; she had "almost continual headaches". Her main fault then was excessive sensitivity: she cried at the least trouble, and once consoled, she cried at having cried. She bore it without complaint, but was sad. Céline never saw in her an outburst or sharp word even then.

### F-EW-105 — The parents' daily routine and Sunday rest
- Claim: Louis and Zélie rose at 5 a.m. to hear Mass every day, fasted all of Lent without relief, and never travelled on Sunday. Louis lost good sales because he would not open his shop on Sunday, though his confessor allowed it and the other jewellers of Alençon did.

- Claim: Their clothes were plain. Thérèse's hair was curled only because their father wanted it. The sisters told Thérèse so often that she was not pretty at all that she became convinced of it.

### F-EW-115 — Timid outside the Buissonnets; hesitant manner hid strength
- Claim: After her mother's death her sensitivity grew at the cost of her vigour. Outside the small family circle she was timid to excess, liked to stay hidden and sincerely thought herself inferior. Only with her sisters was she gay and open. This gave her a hesitant, undecided manner that could deceive people about her energy. Céline never saw her lose her temper or speak sharply.

- Claim: When Céline, as a child, asked how God could be in so small a host, Thérèse, aged four, answered that it was not surprising since He is almighty. From age five she threw flowers before the Blessed Sacrament in Corpus Christi processions.

### F-EW-117 — As a child, she asked Pauline each night if God was pleased
- Claim: As a child it was useless to scold her; it was enough to say she grieved God. Each night before sleep she asked Pauline if God was pleased with her; without a "yes" she would have cried all night. Scruples at 12–13 left her no rest until a heavenly grace removed them.

### F-EW-126 — Louise the maid terrorised Léonie and Hélène
- Claim: The maid Louise could freeze Pauline, Léonie and Hélène with one look; only Marie defied her ("Je suis bien libre moi"). Louise loved Hélène but terrorised her. ### F-EW-127 — Family: the Martin girls' schooling
- Claim: Marie and Pauline were first day-boarders at the Providence school in Alençon, where Marie disliked a teacher who ate sweets, pulled ears and ignored a cut Marie got for her. Marie (from 8) and Pauline then boarded at the Visitation of Le Mans, where their aunt was a nun; holidays were only New Year (8 days), Easter (15 days) and summer (6 weeks to 2 months). Léonie was tried there for three months and sent home as too undisciplined. At home Marie taught Céline every morning (Thérèse sat in from age 3).
- When / where: 1860s–1877
- People: Abbé Boulangé (catechism, Visitation); Sr Marie-Paule (first mistress of the boarding school); Sr Marie-Claire

- Claim: From age two Thérèse seemed more intelligent than her age. Marie found her too serious and too advanced. At their mother's death no one had time for her and she did not seek attention. Marie deliberately did not ask what she was thinking, so as not to feed those deep feelings further.

- Claim: The extreme unction of her mother marked Thérèse deeply. Later in Carmel she told Marie that as a tiny child she judged things as she did now. Marie found her "extraordinarily serious" then, but deliberately did not ask what she thought, because she found her too advanced for her age.

### F-EW-140 — Zélie's death night
- Claim: Zélie wanted no one at night. Marie heard her groans and prayers ("O you who created me, have pity on me!"), once found her dragging herself to a table. Louis asked a nursing sister for nights; Zélie's sad look at her arrival two days before death. Isidore and Céline Guérin came by telegram; Zélie gave her sister-in-law a deep look. Marie promised to care for Léonie. The nursing sister sent Marie to bed; about 11 pm Louis came sobbing: "Your little Mother is no more!" Next day Zélie looked twenty years old.
- When / where: 26–28 Aug 1877, Alençon
- Witness: Marie
- Source: MSC, Cahier

- Claim: At 7 or 8 she dreamed of the hermit life and sometimes shut herself in a corner of her room behind her bed curtains to talk with God. At 14, after her "conversion", she even thought of becoming a sister of the foreign missions, but chose Carmel hoping to save more souls by sacrifice.

### F-EW-167 — The old man who refused her alms
- Claim: As a child she could not bear to see the poor suffer and gave alms with touching respect. She later said that, free with her fortune, she would have ruined herself. Once an infirm old man refused her coin; pained at having hurt him by taking him for a beggar, she wanted to give him her cake; unable to catch him, she resolved to pray for him at her First Communion, four years away.

- Claim: At the Abbey she loved study and was always first in the tests, though her class held older girls, some aged up to 13. - Claim: At school she spent a free quarter of an hour before the sewing lesson in the chapel instead of playing, one of the rare children to do so. Later, returning to the Abbey for sewing lessons, she went to the chapel gallery after class and stayed long hours waiting for her father. She went to Mass each morning.

### F-EW-190 — Wanting to ask the bishop at the station for early first communion
- Claim: Opposition: About age nine, walking in town with her sisters, Thérèse saw the bishop going to the station. She said: "What if I asked His Lordship for permission to make my first communion next year? It is hard to be held back a year for being born on 2 January!" The sisters dissuaded her: it was not proper and he would refuse. Léonie saw her anguish. (The rule required age 10 by 1 January.)

- Claim: Opposition: Domin, the school chaplain, thought — "with many others, I believe" — that Thérèse was too flattered and adulated by her family, above all her father, who seemed unable to part from her and constantly called her "ma petite reine". - Claim: Thérèse suffered from having to wait a year for First Communion, because she was born "two days too late". One day she and Marie met Bishop Hugonin going to the station; she asked Marie whether she could run and ask him for permission. Marie had great trouble holding her back.

- Claim: Meeting Bishop Hugonin in a Lisieux street, the child wanted to run to him and ask leave to make her First Communion early, because the rule made her wait a year for a matter of two days (born 2 January, cut-off 1 January).

- Claim: From a very young age she said, and everyone understood, that she would be a nun. She said she wanted to hide in a desert to belong to God alone. - Claim: One day Thérèse forgot herself and helped a classmate who was saying her lesson badly. The mistress said sharply, "Thérèse has no conscience then!" - Claim: As a young lay mistress, Sr Saint-André was tempted to see Thérèse's exact fidelity as exaggeration and a sign of an anxious, scrupulous soul. She changed her view later. In corridors and stairs, other girls broke silence and played tricks; Thérèse did not.

### F-EW-242 — Symptoms of the 1883 illness: nails, a hat like a beast
- Claim: Violent headaches began just after Pauline left (Oct 1882). At the end of March 1883 came fits of delirium and convulsions. The fits seemed like near-continuous attacks of delirious fright with wild movements; she screamed, her eyes were terrified and her face contracted. Nails in the wall took horrible shapes. One evening she was terrified when her father came near holding his hat, which looked to her like a horrible beast. In convulsions she tried to throw herself over the rail of her bed. The doctor treated her with hydrotherapy, without success.
- When / where: Oct 1882–10 May 1883, Les Buissonnets, Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Louis Martin; the doctor (not named; Dr Notta per other sources, not here)
- Witness: Léonie, SAW
- Source: PO-07 [475r–476r], session 41, 29 Nov 1910
- Original words: « cet objet lui paraissait être une horrible bête »
- Translation: this object seemed to her to be a horrible beast.
- New to English readers: partial (the hat detail is new)
- Confidence: high

### F-EW-289 — Céline's account of the 1883 illness
- Claim: The illness began in Holy Week 1883 and lasted about six weeks. She saw terrifying visions, said things she did not want to say, and seemed to lose use of her senses but never her reason. Her face was pale and almost transparent; in crises she fixed them with a piercing look. When anyone showed fear, the crises got worse. She beat her head on the bed frame, made "strange gymnastic" movements without ever offending modesty, and once threw herself over the bed rail onto the floor without the least harm. Pious objects never repelled her.

### F-EW-2492 — Mère Isabelle du Sacré-Cœur: who she was (editors' note)
- Claim: Yvonne-Ernestine Daurelle was born at Epinac (Saône-et-Loire) on 29 Jan 1882. She thought of entering a Spanish Carmel of St Teresa, but after reading Histoire d'une âme she entered Lisieux on 13 Jan 1904. She took the habit on 21 Jan 1905 and made profession on 19 Mar 1906. She suffered much from ill health and inner trials. She soon helped at the novitiate, was elected sub-prioress on 27 Nov 1909 (per her own deposition), and then acted as novice mistress. She wrote the Vie abrégée of Thérèse (translated into more than twenty languages), prepared volumes III and IV of Pluie de roses (1913, 1914) and a book on the Carmel's foundation (Bar-le-Duc 1912). She died of phthisis on 31 Jul 1914, after the introduction of the cause (10 Jun 1914).
- When / where: 1882–1914, Epinac / Lisieux
- People: Isabelle (Daurelle); her parents Ferdinand Daurelle and Louise Marie Marguerite Falque
- Witness: editors' introduction; her own answer 2 (parents' names, dates)
- Source: PO 16, introduction and [634v], W1
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The editors say she deposed on 20–21 Feb 1911 "in sessions 52–53"; the session headings on the same page say 62 and 63. Treat the session numbers as unresolved.
- Extraction ID: N-F45

### F-EW-2547 — Youf to Thérèse on her deathbed: 'Do not dwell on these thoughts — it is very dangerous'
- Claim: The circular says the chaplain Abbé Youf led souls firmly and was not always good at comforting them. It recalls his reply to Thérèse when, on her deathbed, she spoke of her temptations against faith: 'Do not dwell on those thoughts, it is very dangerous!' After the talk Thérèse sighed patiently: 'You see how little I am consoled!' The circular adds that Youf still admired his 'angelic penitent' without reserve. ### F-EW-2584 — Sr Marie-Dosithée's letters to Isidore: 'I go to God as to my Father' (1870s)
- Claim: Piat quotes letters of the Le Mans Visitandine Sr Marie-Dosithée (Isidore's sister) that he says already contain traits of the Little Way: 'I go to God just as I would to my Father, even with much more trust and abandonment'; 'God loves with an extremely tender love those who abandon themselves to him, and a mother does not have as much tenderness for her small child as the Lord has for the abandoned soul'; 'Our Lord wants no galley slaves in his service.' She sent him the Introduction to the Devout Life and the twelve volumes of L'Année sainte de la Visitation. For his 1875 pilgrimage to Lourdes she wrote that one need not be worthy to receive a miracle: 'the most miserable but with most trust' are heard. Her motto for him: 'I will do God's business and he will do mine.' Before dying she wrote a testament: God alone will be your reward; in prosperity keep your taste and household modest; share your abundance with the poor. Piat sums it up as 'spin the thread of little virtues', the line of the Doctor of Geneva.

### F-EW-2586 — 14 Nov 1877: Isidore Guérin brings the five orphans to Lisieux and finds Les Buissonnets
- Claim: After Zélie's death on 28 Aug 1877, Isidore proposed that Louis Martin move to Lisieux so his daughters could have their aunt nearby. He repeated the offer after the funeral and looked for a house, finding the cottage of Les Buissonnets by the Parc de l'Étoile. On Wednesday 14 Nov 1877 he himself brought the five nieces to Lisieux (Louis followed later, after closing his business). Marie was 17½, Pauline 16, Léonie 14, Céline 8½ and Thérèse would be five on 2 Jan. Piat says the two families 'fused' but not in the same climate: the Guérins were less strict, spoiled Jeanne a little, and Marie was a merry child who could talk herself out of trouble.

### F-EW-2587 — The cousins' routines: school walks, Thursdays, Sundays at the pharmacy, holidays by the sea
- Claim: Piat says Céline Martin became Jeanne's chosen friend while Marie attached herself to Thérèse, three years her junior. They went to the Benedictine school, meeting at the Place Saint-Pierre under the eye of the two families' maids, Victoire and Marcelline. On Thursdays they often joined the Maudelonde girls (children of Mme Maudelonde, Céline Guérin's sister); on Sundays the Martin girls took turns to be invited to the pharmacy, and Louis came to fetch them in the evening. The grandmother Fournet had a modest country house at Saint-Ouen-le-Pin, reached in a hired break. In 1878, 1885, 1886 and 1887 Isidore rented on the coast for one or two months (the chalet Colombe at Deauville, the villa Marie-Rose, the chalet des Lilas at Trouville) and invited the nieces in turn; Thérèse went several times. Thérèse wrote how, wishing to attract notice by imitating Marie's chronic migraines, she only repeated the fable of the donkey and the lapdog: complaining suited her so badly that people thought her tears hid a scruple.

### F-EW-2594 — Isidore Guérin as a notable of Lisieux: reading, politics and the family papers
- Claim: Piat says that from 1888 Isidore lived as a 'notable': a member of the Literary Circle since 1869, he read aloud in the evenings to his daughters and nieces Corneille, Racine, Molière and Shakespeare, but not Voltaire. He knew leading politicians, was an inveterate monarchist and a reader of Drumont's La Libre Parole. His archivist's instinct made him collect and file the family papers, his 'cahier' being a livre de raison, and to him posterity owes many catalogued letters of Mme Martin and her daughters. He founded (1885, at Louis Martin's urging) the Nocturnal Adoration group; at Corpus Christi he held a cord of the canopy and built a lavish altar, once with a coloured-glass cross inscribed 'The more they insult it, the more it shines'. His purse was open to the poor and the missions, which made him godfather to a 'black king'.
- When / where: 1885–1896, Lisieux
- People: Isidore Guérin; Louis Martin; Paul-Louis Target

### F-EW-2596 — Henry Chéron: the anticlerical who knew Thérèse as a child, and later built the road to the Basilica
- Claim: Piat's footnote says Chéron, later a mayor, deputy and minister, dropped his sectarianism and kept real esteem for Isidore. As mayor of Lisieux he eased the great events in her honour and the development of pilgrimages, and was responsible for the triumphal avenue leading to the Basilica. ### F-LF-017 — Point d'Alençon: what the lace was
- Claim: Point d'Alençon is needle lace made of very fine hand-spun linen thread, in small pieces (20–30 cm) joined by invisible stitches; the industry was started at Alençon about 1664 under Colbert with 30 workers from Venice. Zélie specialised as an "assembleuse" (the worker who joins the pieces). Her workers worked at home; she took orders, supplied designs, checked the work and did the final joining; 500 francs a metre was a normal price for her lace.
- When / where: 1850s–1877; Alençon
- People: Zélie and her outworkers
- Source: LAV p.29–30 and fn.
- Source type: early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- Other sources: Carmel "Le couple Martin": she kept her "bureau" open on Thursdays to deliver, receive and pay work; she usually repaired the tulle and did the joining herself. FR Wikipedia (via helper notes): up to about twenty workers.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high (process); medium (price and workforce)
- Notes: The number of workers is not given by Laveille or the Carmel; "about twenty" is Wikipedia only.

- When / where: letter of 13 Feb 1877; rue Saint-Blaise

### F-LF-085 — Léonie's childhood and the servant Louise Marais
- Claim: Léonie (b. 3 Jun 1863) was a sickly, slow and difficult child, sent back several times from the Le Mans school (her aunt called her a "terrible little girl"). For years she obeyed only the family servant, Louise Marais, and avoided her mother. In March 1877 Marie discovered that the servant had been secretly mistreating Léonie, who dared not complain "for fear of getting it double". Zélie: "brutality never converted anyone; it only makes slaves." Léonie now clung to her mother and "is now like a normal child". Zélie called it "the greatest grief I have ever had in my life".

## Part 4 — Zélie's cancer and death (1865–1877)
### F-LF-094 — Winding up the lace business
- Claim: In May 1877 Zélie wrote: "I have given up in good earnest my point d'Alençon and now begin to live on my income … it has cost me dear." In fact she kept finishing orders almost to the end: in late July she was still completing four orders and had taken back 11 metres; she had refused 18 metres of flounce the month before and had 24 metres started "for my own account or for Mme Deverny". Isidore scolded her for working. Louis was still finishing the last pieces with the "assembleuses" in Nov 1877.
- When / where: May–Nov 1877; Alençon

### F-LF-095 — A doctor who "believes in nothing"
- Claim: When she went back to "Dr X" in June 1877 he was curt: "You know we have given it up." When she said the tumour would heal "when I am dead", he said it was not impossible she would recover — in an "indifferent, bored tone, with a sneer". She wrote that he "believes in nothing", that she threw his last prescription into the fire unread, and yet that the day he told her the whole truth "that consultation is priceless to me".
- When / where: letter of 14 Jun 1877

### F-LF-096 — The Lourdes pilgrimage, June 1877
- Claim: Zélie joined the Angers diocesan pilgrimage to Lourdes (train from Angers 18 Jun 1877, 7.50 a.m.), taking Marie, Pauline and Léonie; Louis at first disapproved of taking all three, then wished it. The journey went badly: leaking water cans, lodging changed by Abbé Martignon to the Sisters of Nevers' orphanage, Marie with grit in her eye for four hours, Léonie's swollen feet, Léonie sleepwalking in the night train and throwing herself on her mother and against the carriage door. At the piscina she plunged into "icy water and marble cold as death" and nearly choked; she went in four times. She came home with five francs, and lost her dead sister's rosary, "the only relic I had".
- When / where: 17–23 Jun 1877; Le Mans, Angers, Lourdes
- People: Zélie; Marie; Pauline; Léonie; Sr Marie-Paula (Visitation of Angers); Abbé Martignon

- Rights: public-domain (letters)

## Part 5 — Les Buissonnets, Lisieux (1877–1881)
### F-LF-109 — Arrival at Lisieux, November 1877
- Claim: Isidore brought the five girls to Lisieux in mid-November 1877; Mme Guérin and her daughters met them at the door of the Guérin house (corner of the Grande-Rue and today's Place Thiers, with the pharmacy); after a night there they went to Les Buissonnets. Marie wrote to her father on 16 Nov: "We are installed at Les Buissonnets … Only the staircase and the approach to the house leave something to be desired." Louis stayed in Alençon to finish the lace orders and arrived on Friday 30 Nov at about 7.30 p.m.
- When / where: 15–30 Nov 1877; Lisieux

### F-LF-110 — The house and its rooms
- Claim: Les Buissonnets stood half-way up a hill east of Lisieux, reached from the Pont-l'Évêque road by a steep winding path Louis called "the way to Paradise"; red brick, low ceilings, far from the church; a lawn with trees in front, a garden with an ivy-covered wall behind. Céline and Thérèse shared a ground-floor room opening on the back garden; Louis had the top-floor "Belvedere" for reading and prayer; Léonie's room was a narrow passage between her sisters' rooms. During her 1883 illness Thérèse was moved to Marie's front room with two large windows.
- When / where: 1877–1889; Les Buissonnets, Lisieux

### F-LF-111 — The servants: Victoire Pasquer and others
- Claim: Victoire Pasquer (1857–1935), hired through the Guérins, served at Les Buissonnets from Nov 1877 until 1884; the child told her once, "You know well, Victoire, that this offends the good God," when she told small lies in fun; Victoire was the only "congregation" at Thérèse's May altar made on a chest of drawers with wax vestas for candles. Later servants included Félicité (1885) and, from 1892, Désiré and Marie Le Juif.

### F-LF-112 — Louis's day at Lisieux
- Claim: At Lisieux Louis lived on his investments ("rentes"). He went daily to early Mass at the cathedral of Saint-Pierre — first at 6 a.m. "with the poor", whom he called God's favourite children, then, at his elder daughters' insistence and "with regret", at 7; walked home silent ("I continue to commune with our Lord"); gardened; read and meditated in the Belvedere; made a second daily visit to the Blessed Sacrament with Thérèse, which is how she first entered the Carmel chapel; fished in the Touques. He persuaded Isidore to found a Nocturnal Adoration society with the clergy of Saint-Pierre and joined the St Vincent de Paul Society.
- When / where: 1877–1887; Lisieux
- People: Louis; Thérèse; Isidore
- Source: LAV p.100–102, p.113; Carmel "Le couple Martin"
- Source type: early-life / scholarship
- Rights: public-domain / unknown
- New to English readers: yes

### F-LF-113 — Pauline as teacher: work first, then the walk
- Claim: At Les Buissonnets Marie and Pauline kept house and taught Thérèse. Marie taught her writing and Pauline taught the rest (Carmel schooling page). Walks were the reward for good work. Pauline cancelled a walk when the work was poor, and Louis always confirmed the ban. At about three and a half Thérèse stopped learning to read for a time. The first word she read alone was "cieux" (heavens), and she ran to tell her father in the Belvedere.
- When / where: 1877–1881; Les Buissonnets
- People: Pauline; Marie; Louis; Thérèse

- Claim: Pauline taught the child about degrees of glory in heaven. She filled a thimble and Louis's tumbler with water and asked which was fuller. Both were full, and neither could hold more. So, Pauline said, in heaven each person is full, though some have more glory than others.

- Claim: At seven she followed the preparation for Céline's First Communion (Céline was a day-boarder at the Abbey) until she was told she was too young. On Christmas Eve she said to Marie that she would like to slip in among the others: "I am so small that no one would notice me."

### F-LF-124 — First sight of the sea, Trouville, August 1878
- Claim: In August 1878, when she was nearly six, Thérèse went to Trouville with Louis, her sisters and the Guérins. It was the first time she saw the sea. She sat with Pauline on a rock. She wrote later that the golden path of the sun on the water was like grace, and her heart was a small boat with a white sail (HA ch. ii p.35). A lady on the beach said, "What a pretty little girl!" and Louis signed to her not to compliment the child. A family friend said, "she has heaven in her eyes". Louis then had white hair and a face "still young".

### F-LF-128 — The vision of the veiled man
- Claim: Thérèse wrote that, while her father was away (at Alençon, on business, and not due back soon), she stood alone at a window over the kitchen garden at about 2–3 p.m. on a sunny day. By the wash-house she saw a man dressed like her father, of his height and gait, but "very bent and aged", with a thick veil over his head. He walked slowly past her little garden toward a clump of fir trees and did not come out again. She cried "Papa! Papa!" Marie, in the next room, was frightened and asked why she called for papa when he was at Alençon. The sisters suggested the maid with an apron over her head. Victoire said she had not left the kitchen. They searched behind the trees and found no one. Marie told her to think no more about it. Thérèse wrote that she understood the meaning only after fourteen years.
- When / where: 1879 or 1880; Les Buissonnets
- People: Thérèse; Marie; Pauline (in the next room, per Laveille); Victoire Pasquer; Louis (absent)
- Source: LAV p.115–116 (HA ch. ii p.32); Marie's preparatory notes (Carmel page: https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur/notes-preparatoires-de-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur/); HA 1898 text (local D1a, lines 2294–2345, checked by helper)

### F-LF-130 — Entry to the Abbey school, October 1881
- Claim: Léonie was a boarder at the Benedictine abbey of Notre-Dame-du-Pré from early 1878 to the 1881 holidays, and Céline a half-boarder from early 1878 to mid-1885. Louis was satisfied and placed Thérèse there as a half-boarder on 3 Oct 1881, aged eight and a half. Laveille says about sixty pupils, from the town and "the best families of the neighbourhood", and that farmers' daughters and refined town girls caused friction. He describes high grey walls, austere classrooms, a chapel of Louis-Philippe's time and a walled garden. Thérèse returned every evening to Les Buissonnets. The Carmel school page says one year in the green class, two in the violet and two in the orange.
- When / where: 3 Oct 1881; Notre-Dame-du-Pré, Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Céline; Léonie; Louis
- Source: LAV p.120–121 (footnote: "manuscript notice on the Abbey"); Carmel schooling page https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/environnement-familial/scolarite-des-filles/scolarite-des-filles-martin/ ; Carmel place page https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/lieux/abbaye-benedictine-notre-dame-du-pre/ (both via helper, web-context [T7])

### F-LF-131 — The walk to school and the Guérin servant
- Claim: Each morning Céline and Thérèse called at the Guérin pharmacy and walked to the Abbey with cousins Jeanne and Marie Guérin, led by a Guérin servant. That servant later became a Benedictine, Sr Joséphine de la Croix, and testified at the Process. She said the child, alone with her, was "affectionate and confiding" and spoke of spiritual things. Before her First Communion Thérèse heard workmen blaspheme and excused them: they had received much less grace and were "more unfortunate than blameworthy".
- When / where: 1881–1884; Lisieux
- People: Sr Joséphine de la Croix OSB; Jeanne and Marie Guérin; Céline; Thérèse

### F-LF-132 — In class: older girls, "little Doctor", father's nightly check
- Claim: Thérèse was put at once in a class of much older girls, some fourteen. Some equalled her in arithmetic and spelling; she soon ranked "amongst the first". She loved Sacred History and Church History. Reciting the catechism word for word was at first hard. The chaplain Abbé Domin taught religion and called her "his little Doctor" for exact answers. Louis looked at her marks each evening, and she was "inconsolable" when she got fewer marks in religion, because he would have less joy.

### F-LF-141 — The cure, Pentecost Sunday 13 May 1883 (Thérèse's account)
- Claim: Thérèse wrote that during the novena, on a Sunday, Marie went to the garden and left her with Léonie by the window. She called "Marie" in a whisper, then loudly. Marie came, and for the first time Thérèse did not know her. Marie whispered to Léonie and left, "pale and trembling". Léonie carried her to the window. Marie held out her arms in the garden and Thérèse did not know her. Marie knelt by the bed before the statue of Mary, with Léonie and Céline. Thérèse turned to it too. She wrote: "All at once the statue became animated… her ravishing smile. Then all my pain vanished; two big tears gushed from my eyes." She then knew Marie "without effort". She decided to tell no one.

### F-LF-142 — Marie's testimony and the statue
- Claim: At the Process Marie said the "most terrible crisis" had come and she thought Thérèse would die. She and her sisters knelt at the statue. She said the same prayer three times. - Claim: Thérèse went back to the Abbey. Marie, who had taken over teaching after Pauline left, prepared her each evening in Thérèse's room or the garden. ### F-LF-148 — First Communion, 8 May 1884
- Claim: Thérèse received First Communion at the Abbey on 8 May 1884, with chaplain Abbé Domin. She wrote of "the joyous awakening", the dressing room "filled with snowy vesture", and the hymn "O sacred altar girt with angel-guard". She wrote: "Ah, how sweet it was, this first kiss of Jesus to my soul! … We were no longer two; Thérèse had disappeared like the drop of water which is lost in the ocean" (HA ch. iv p.58). She wept, and companions said it was because she missed her mother or her Carmelite sister (HA p.59). That evening she wrote three resolutions: never to give way to discouragement, to say the Memorare every day, and to try to humble her pride (Léonie's deposition, Process of the Ordinary p.132). In the afternoon she read the Act of Consecration to Mary for all the communicants. Louis gave her a watch. Pauline made her profession the same day, and after the ceremony Louis took Thérèse to the Carmel to see Pauline in a white veil and crown of roses. Thérèse felt envy, not pain.

### F-LF-153 — The headache and "the Ass and the Dog"
- Claim: At Trouville cousin Marie Guérin, about fourteen, had a headache; her mother fondled her and got only tears. A few days later Thérèse, who had frequent headaches and never complained, sank on a chair in a corner of the sitting-room and cried. Asked why, she said, "I have a headache." No one sympathised. Her aunt reproved her gravely for playing the spoiled child. Jeanne, "slightly sarcastic", said Thérèse lacked confidence and simplicity and was probably tormented by a big scruple (Abbé Lepetit, *La Croix*, Nov 1913). Thérèse wrote that she understood the fable "The Ass and the Dog": she was the ass who put his hoof on the table to be kissed like the lap-dog, and the price "cured me for ever of the desire to attract attention" (HA ch. iv p.67).

### F-LF-155 — Louis's journey to Germany, Austria, Constantinople and Italy, 1885
- Claim: At the wish of Abbé Marie, curate of Saint-Jacques, Lisieux, Louis toured Germany, Austria, Constantinople and Italy for about six weeks in Aug–Oct 1885 and wrote to his daughters. Dates from his letters: Paris 2 Aug; Munich 27 Aug; Vienna 30 Aug; Black Sea 7 Sep; Constantinople 11 and 16 Sep (Sultan, dervishes, Hagia Sophia, the bazaar; per family-letters notes); Naples 25 Sep (Pompeii); Rome 27 Sep; Milan 6 Oct. He gave pet names: Marie "my Diamond", Pauline "my fine Pearl", Céline "the Intrepid", Thérèse "ma Reine de France et de Navarre" and "ma petite Reine". From Vienna (30 Aug) he wrote he seemed to see them in the Belvedere and hear "Thérèse's sweet and sympathetic voice"; in the same letter he wrote: "Vanity of vanities, and all is vanity, except loving God and serving Him!" From Rome (27 Sep) he wrote to Marie: "St Peter's is for me the most beautiful thing in the world" and "how sad that the Holy Father (Leo XIII) is thus in captivity"; and, of Pauline, "Tell my 'Fine Pearl' that I am too happy, and that 'I offer my back', for this cannot last" ("je tends le dos"). From Milan (6 Oct): "Everything I see is splendid, but it is always an earthly beauty and our heart is satisfied with nothing until it sees the infinite beauty which is God… Soon the intimate pleasure of family; it is this beauty that brings us nearest."

- People: Louis; Abbé Marie; Marie; Pauline; Céline; Thérèse

- Claim: Abbey classes reopened in October 1885 and Thérèse went alone, since Céline had finished. Scruples had "redoubled", and headaches were so bad that after a few months (early 1886) Louis took her out. She then went several times a week to "a lady of good position" for lessons. She sat at a desk in an old-fashioned room where ladies came daily for distraction; one admired her hair, another whispered who the pretty child was, and she feared for her humility. The Carmel names the teacher as Mme Papinau (widow Valentine Cochain, 1835–1898), of the Grande Rue, and dates the lessons in one place from 21 Sep 1886 to March 1888, and on her person page from March 1886 to February 1888.

### F-LF-157 — Children of Mary at the Abbey, 31 May 1887
- Claim: To join the Sodality of the Children of Mary, Thérèse accepted a period of probation among older pupils, with a manual-work lesson at the Abbey two or three times a week. Mother Saint-Placide, head of the boarding-school, said on receiving her request, "We shall never have to regret her name on our list." Thérèse came without enthusiasm and wrote that, because no one noticed her, she went up to the chapel tribune until her father came (HA ch. iv p.68). An older girl, later Sr Saint-Jean l'Évangéliste, said Thérèse stayed before the tabernacle "usually more than an hour". Once this girl teased her about her sincerity, and Thérèse said, "Marie, I never tell a lie." She was received on 31 May 1887. Soon after entering Carmel she wrote to Mother Saint-Placide (letter in Laveille's appendix).

### F-LF-158 — Marie enters Carmel; the scruples end
- Claim: In October 1886 Marie, Thérèse's "principal confidante", carried out her plan to join Pauline at Carmel (entry 15 Oct 1886). Thérèse wept and asked who would now guide her in troubles of conscience. She prayed to her four brothers and sisters who died in infancy, and asked for peace of mind, to prove that "up there they still know how to love". Laveille says a "delicious peace" filled her and the scruples ceased. Her "excessive sensitiveness" and floods of tears remained.
- When / where: Oct 1886; Lisieux
- People: Marie; Pauline; Thérèse; the four dead siblings

- Notes: Connection: the "little heaven children" (see Part 4 cards).
### F-LF-159 — Christmas 1886: the shoes and "too childish"
- Claim: Thérèse wrote that after midnight Mass she expected her shoes on the hearth filled with gifts, as in her "infant days", proof that she had been "treated like a little baby". Louis, "contrary to his usual habit of indulging me in everything", was annoyed. As she went up the stairs she heard him say, "For a big girl like Thérèse, this is too childish; I hope this will be the last time." Céline said in a low voice, "Do not go down immediately; wait a little; you would surely cry when looking at the presents before papa." Thérèse drove back her tears, went down and took gifts from the shoes "as happy as a queen". Louis laughed and Céline "thought she was in a dream". She wrote that Jesus "accomplished the work that I had failed to do in many years" and she had regained her strength of soul lost at four and a half (HA ch. v p.75). "The fountain of my tears was dried up."
- When / where: 25 Dec 1886; Les Buissonnets
- People: Thérèse; Louis; Céline
- Source: LAV p.146–148 (HA ch. v p.75)
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline: "Christmas 1886 conversion")
- Confidence: high for Thérèse's account
- Notes: Only Thérèse, Louis and Céline are named as present in Laveille's text. Louis's words are as Thérèse heard them from the stairs.
### F-LF-160 — "What I have loved": a Carmel poem on Les Buissonnets days
- Claim: Laveille quotes a poem written in Carmel after a Chateaubriand model, "What I have Loved". It begins "Oh, well-beloved memory, The joyous days of infancy!" and lists the wheatfield gleaning with her sisters, the white daisy, the Sunday walk, the evening meal, the perfume of the rose, "the summer-day at Buissonnets".
- When / where: written in Carmel (date not in LAV); about 1877–1886

- Source: LAV p.148

- Rights: public-domain

## Part 7 — Vocation, opposition and the Rome pilgrimage (1887–1888)
### F-LF-161 — Léonie's first tries at religious life, 1886–1888
- Claim: On 7 Oct 1886, during a family stay at Alençon, Léonie went to the Poor Clares' parlour to ask about entry and was received at once, already dressed as a postulant. Louis fetched her out on 1 Dec 1886 after seven weeks (eczema, poor health). She entered the Visitation at Caen on 16 Jul 1887 and left on 6 Jan 1888; her father met her in the parlour.
- When / where: Oct 1886 – Jan 1888; Alençon; Caen
- People: Léonie; Louis

### F-LF-163 — "The joy of the family": Léonie's testimony and the poor children
- Claim: Léonie testified that Thérèse, "constantly at home", was "in truth the joy of the family". The servants loved her. She forgot herself to please others, and her even temper seemed so natural that no one thought it cost her. Thérèse later said she had great compassion for servants and thought of heaven, where the poor would be repaid for their humiliations. The Guérin servant testified that at scarcely fourteen Thérèse visited poor little girls and taught them their catechism. Thérèse wrote that, when a mother was ill, she looked after two children, the elder six, and spoke to them of the reward of the Little Jesus instead of promising toys (HA ch. v p.87). When Mme Guérin's servant said she felt no love for God, Thérèse told her that love does not lie in feeling but in the practice of virtue.
- When / where: 1886–1887; Lisieux
- People: Léonie; Sr Joséphine de la Croix; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.151–153 (Léonie, Apostolic Process; Joséphine, Apostolic Process p.140; HA p.87)
- Source type: eyewitness / own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The passage on the servant's lack of love has no footnote in Laveille.
### F-LF-191 — Marie de Gonzague: portrait of the prioress
- Claim: In April 1888 Mère Marie de Gonzague was in the second year of a fourth three-year term, succeeding Mère Geneviève. Laveille says she was born at Caen in 1834 of a noble Calvados family, with initiative, administrative talent, "a great heart and a charming personality", but "very impressionable, and of somewhat distrustful character, predisposed to melancholy", without always "even-balanced judgement". He says she had desired Thérèse's entry but judged a vocation so early needed testing, and "with a certain natural impulsiveness not always held in check" made the child's early life painful. She was the same nun who "built the second wing" in 1876 (FND p.12). She was elected again in 1896.
- When / where: 1886–1904; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague (baptismal name Rosalie, per web-context); Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.199–200; FND p.12
- Source type: early-life / old-collection
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — Laveille's judgement, written with the Carmel's approval.
- Notes: Her death in 1904: F-LF-243.
### F-LF-256 — The 1880 decrees against the unauthorised congregations
- Claim: On 29 Mar 1880 Jules Ferry (Minister of Public Instruction since Feb 1879) promoted two decrees: the Jesuits were to be expelled from France, and other religious congregations had three months to ask authorisation, or face dissolution and dispersion. Most congregations refused to ask, in solidarity with the Jesuits, and the unauthorised ones, among them Benedictines, Capuchins, Carmes (men), Franciscans and Assumptionists, were expelled. Some Dominican houses were closed and some anticlerical town councils removed nursing sisters from hospitals. About 200 members of the public prosecutors' offices resigned rather than carry out the measure.
- When / where: 29 Mar 1880 and after; France
- People: Jules Ferry
- Source: FR Wikipedia, "Histoire de la laïcité en France", section on Jules Ferry https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histoire_de_la_la%C3%AFcit%C3%A9_en_France (raw page text read)
- Source type: scholarship
- Rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high — one encyclopedia page, no primary text read.
- Notes: The page speaks of male "Carmes"; I found no source saying that the nuns of the Lisieux Carmel were affected in 1880 (gap). Thérèse was seven. Isidore Guérin was described by Laveille as "a formidable polemical opponent of the Church's enemies" (LAV p.97; see F-LF-245).
### F-LF-257 — The Ferry school laws, 1881–1882
- Claim: On 16 Jun 1881 the first Jules Ferry law made primary education free for boys and girls; a law of 1882 made it compulsory and secular. Members of Catholic orders could no longer teach in public schools after a five-year transition. The dual system of state and church schools staffed by religious was replaced by state schools and lay teachers. The laws are often described as part of a wider anticlerical campaign.
- When / where: 16 Jun 1881; 1882; France
- People: Jules Ferry
- Source: EN Wikipedia, "Jules Ferry laws" https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Ferry_laws (raw page text read)
- Source type: scholarship
- Rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the two dates and main terms
- Notes: Thérèse entered the Benedictine abbey school, a private Catholic school, on 3 Oct 1881, four months after the first law (F-LF-130). The Martin girls did not go to a state school; the sources I read do not comment on the laws.

### F-OM-002 — The official texts say she lost her mother at four years and seven months and was raised by Marie, Pauline and her father
- Claim: The bull says she was barely four years and seven months old when her mother died; that she "lost all sense of joy"; and that her education was entrusted to her elder sisters Marie and Pauline, under her father's watchful care.
- When / where: Aug 1877
- People: Marie Martin; Pauline Martin; Louis Martin
- Source: AAS 17 p. 338
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « magno dolore correpta, omnem iucunditatis sensum amisit »
- Translation: "seized by great sorrow, she lost all sense of joy"
- Other sources: Office lesson IV (AAS 20 p. 148) says "in her fifth year". Benedict XV (allocution, Taylor 1926/30 PDF 289) says witnesses affirm the loss "produced in her a seriousness and maturity of mind far in advance of her years".
- New to English readers: partial (baseline has the death; not this wording)
- Confidence: high
### F-OM-003 — The school chaplain called her "the little doctor"
- Claim: The bull says that at the Benedictine school in Lisieux, where she spent the whole day and came home at evening, she was younger than the other pupils but ahead of them, and the nuns' chaplain called her "teacher of religion" or "little doctor". It adds that she later knew the whole *Imitation of Christ* by heart.
- When / where: c. 1881–1886, Abbey of Notre-Dame-du-Pré, Lisieux
- People: the chaplain of the Benedictine nuns (not named)
- Source: AAS 17 p. 338
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « ut a sacerdote, earumdem monialium cappellano, ipsa "Religionis magistra" seu "parvus Doctor" appellaretur »
- Translation: "so that the priest who was chaplain of these nuns called her 'mistress of religion' or 'little doctor'"
- Other sources: The beatification brief (AAS 15 p. 202) says she learned the catechism so well "that she was called the little teacher" (« ut parva magistra appellaretur ») and that she retained the *Imitation* "to the letter" and knew the Scriptures well. Taylor's English of the bull (1926/30 PDF 307–308): "his 'theologian', or the 'little doctor'".
- New to English readers: yes — a striking detail given the 1997 doctorate
- Confidence: high for what the documents say; the documents do not name their witness.
- Notes: The documents do not mention the bullying at school that the baseline reports.
### F-OM-004 — The beatification brief says the family thought her childhood illness came from the devil
- Claim: The brief *Quod Ioannes* says that at about ten she fell gravely ill "with an illness whose nature escaped even the doctors", that her family held the opinion ("not without reason") that it was sent by the devil, who foresaw the harm she would do him, and that her sudden, wonderful cure after prayers to the Virgin confirmed that opinion.
- When / where: 1883, Les Buissonnets
- Source: AAS 15 p. 202
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « fuitque non inconsulta familiarium opinio, infirmitatem illam fuisse mali daemonis arte immissam »
- Translation: "and it was the not unreasonable opinion of her family that this illness had been brought on by the craft of the evil spirit"
- Other sources: The bull (AAS 17 p. 338) says a "mysterious and grave illness" tormented her for a long time in her tenth year, and she was freed, "as she herself related", by the Virgin, who appeared to her smiling, after a novena to Our Lady of Victories. Office lesson IV (AAS 20 p. 149) repeats this. See F-OM-005 for how the Congregation handled the illness.
- New to English readers: yes (the devil explanation in an official papal brief)
- Confidence: high for the text

### F-OW-010 — Dream of two little devils on a barrel of lime
- Claim: At about four she dreamed that two ugly little devils danced on a barrel of lime in the garden, despite flat-irons on their feet; when they saw her they fled in fright into the laundry room and ran about on the tables, peering out to see if she was still there. She later read it as proof that devils are "cowards" who flee from a child's look.

A shopkeeper asked what the child meant by always talking about "pratiques" (little acts of virtue) in the garden; Mme Gaucherin leaned out of her window to understand the debate. Zélie described Thérèse reciting a poem with eyes raised "with an angelic expression".

### F-OW-013 — Her mother's last weeks: sent each day to Mme Leriche's
- Claim: In the last weeks of Zélie's illness, Mme Leriche came each morning to fetch Céline and Thérèse, who spent the day at her house "like poor little exiles". One day they had not said their prayers; Mme Leriche put them in a big room and left: "Ah, it's not like Mama... she always had us say our prayers!" Céline saved an apricot to give to their mother, who was already too ill to eat.

### F-OW-014 — Her mother's death: the last kiss and the coffin lid
- Claim: At the extreme unction the five daughters stood in order of age and their father sobbed. The day of the death or the next day, Louis took her in his arms: "Come and kiss your poor little Mother one last time." She put her lips to her mother's forehead without a word. She says she did not cry much and spoke to no one of her feelings; no one had time for her, so she saw things they would have hidden. She stood a long time looking at the coffin lid, which seemed very big; fifteen years later, at Mère Geneviève's coffin (Dec 1891), the same size, she felt a child again.
- When / where: 28 Aug 1877, Alençon
- People: Louis Martin; her four sisters; Zélie
- Source: Ms A 12r–12v

- Rights: public-domain

### F-OW-015 — "Well, for me, Pauline will be Mama!"
- Claim: After the funeral the five sisters stood together; the maid Louise said, "Poor little things, you have no mother any more!" Céline threw herself into Marie's arms: "Well, you'll be Mama." Thérèse, who usually copied Céline, turned instead to Pauline: "Well, for me, Pauline will be Mama!"

- Claim: Thérèse wrote that from her mother's death "my happy character changed completely; I, so lively, so outgoing, became timid and gentle, oversensitive. A look was enough to make me burst into tears." She could not bear strangers and was happy only in the family. She dates this "second period" from age four and a half to fourteen, "the most painful of the three".

### F-OW-017 — Arrival at Lisieux; she was afraid of her uncle
- Claim: She felt no grief leaving Alençon; "children like change". She remembers arriving in the evening at her aunt's, with Jeanne and Marie waiting at the door. She loved her aunt and "above all my uncle, only he frightened me", and she was not at ease at his house as at Les Buissonnets. He sat her on one knee and sang "Bluebeard" in a formidable voice.

### F-OW-018 — Daily life at Les Buissonnets: lessons, walks, the "tisanes"
- Claim: Each morning Pauline asked if she had given her heart to God, dressed her and prayed with her. The first word she read alone was "Cieux" (Heavens). Marie taught writing; Pauline the rest. Grammar made her cry ("Remember the masculine and feminine!"). After lessons she climbed to the belvedere to show her father her rosette and mark ("5 sans exception"). Every afternoon she walked with her father to visit the Blessed Sacrament in a different church; this is how she first entered the Carmel chapel. She made "herbal teas" from seeds and bark and brought them to her father, who pretended to drink and then asked, as if in secret, whether to throw it away. She built little altars in the garden wall and made him shut his eyes until she said "Papa, open your eyes!"
- When / where: 1878–1881, Les Buissonnets, Lisieux
- People: Pauline; Marie; Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 13v–14v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « le premier mot que je pus lire seule fut celui-ci : «Cieux» »
- Translation: "The first word I could read by myself was this: 'Heavens'."
- Other sources: The edited text keeps the tisanes and altars (ch. II).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-019 — Fishing days with her father; the storm; carried on his back
- Claim: Her father took her fishing; she tried with a little line but preferred to sit alone on the grass, where "without knowing what it was to meditate, my soul sank into real prayer". She heard the distant music of the soldiers. Her jam sandwich turned a faded pink by afternoon. Once a thunderstorm came; she was delighted, not afraid, "God seemed so close". Her father, afraid the wet grass would soak her, carried her on his back with all his fishing gear.
- When / where: c. 1878–1881, around Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 14v–15r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain

- Translation: "the fine slice of bread and jam ... had changed its look"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-020 — The beggar who refused her sou
- Claim: On a walk she offered a sou to a man on crutches; he did not think himself poor enough, smiled sadly and refused. She wanted to give him her cake but did not dare. She decided to pray for him on her First Communion day, since she had heard that one obtains everything then; she kept the promise five years later.
- When / where: c. 1879, Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin; an unnamed poor man
- Source: Ms A 15r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain

### F-OW-021 — Temper with Victoire the maid: stamping her foot; "Victoire, you're a brat!"
- Claim: Thérèse describes two scenes with the maid Victoire (Pasquer). (1) In May, at her little home altar lit by two tiny wax matches, Victoire laughed and would not start the Memorare; Thérèse stood up, told her loudly she was "wicked" and stamped her foot with all her strength; Victoire then showed the candle-ends she had brought. Thérèse cried tears of anger, then of repentance. (2) Victoire refused to hand down an inkwell and told her to climb on a chair; Thérèse did so, then, before jumping down, turned "with dignity" and said: "Victoire, you're a brat!" (« mioche », the word Victoire used for her). Victoire shouted in dialect: "M'amz'elle Mâri... Thérasse vient d'me dire que j'suis une mioche!" Marie made her apologise, "but I did it without contrition".
- When / where: c. 1879–1881, Les Buissonnets
- People: Victoire Pasquer (maid); Marie
- Source: Ms A 15v–16r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Victoire, vous êtes une mioche! »
- Translation: "Victoire, you're a little brat!"
- Other sources: The 1911 edited text keeps the altar-candle story (ch. II) but omits the "mioche" episode entirely.
- New to English readers: yes (the "mioche" scene); partial (the altar scene)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Victoire's surname is not in the manuscript; it comes from other sources (check stage 3). Humor and temper together.
### F-OW-022 — She fell into a bucket and into the fireplace
- Claim: Rocking on her chair while Victoire ironed, Thérèse fell backwards into a bucket of water: "my feet touched my head and I filled the bucket like a little chick fills its egg"; she was stuck and Victoire had to pull her out and change all her clothes, "soaked like a soup". Another time she fell into the (unlit) fireplace and Victoire shook the ashes off her. These things happened on Wednesdays when Pauline and Marie were at singing lessons.
- When / where: c. 1879–1881, Les Buissonnets
- People: Victoire
- Source: Ms A 16r–16v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je remplissais le seau comme un petit poulet remplit son oeuf !... »

- Other sources: Absent from the 1911 edited text (phrase checks "fond du seau", "tombai dans la cheminée").
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-023 — First confession: too small to be seen
- Claim: Pauline prepared her; Thérèse asked whether she should tell M. Ducellier she loved him with all her heart, since she would be speaking to God in him. In the confessional her head was below the arm-rest, so the priest could not see her and told her to stand; she stood facing him "to see him well" and confessed "like a big girl". She felt lighter and happier than ever before.

- People: Abbé Ducellier (her confessor); Pauline
- Source: Ms A 16v
