- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-024 — Sundays: chocolate in bed, curls, and her father in church
- Claim: On Sundays she stayed in bed longer, Pauline brought her chocolate, Marie curled her hair ("not always nice when her hair was pulled"). In church she sat next to her father; people gave up their seats to see "such a handsome old man with such a little girl". Her uncle called her his "little ray of sunshine". When a preacher spoke of St Teresa, her father whispered, "Listen well, my little queen, they are speaking of your patron saint." She watched her father more than the preacher; his eyes sometimes filled with tears. By Compline on Sunday evening she felt sad that the day of rest was ending.
- When / where: c. 1878–1881, Lisieux (Saint-Pierre cathedral)
- People: Louis Martin; Pauline; Marie; Isidore Guérin
- Source: Ms A 17r–17v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Écoute bien, ma petite reine, on parle de ta Ste Patronne. »
- Translation: "Listen carefully, my little queen, they're talking about your patron saint."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-025 — Her name written in the stars; fear of the dark
- Claim: Walking home at night she saw a group of stars shaped like a T and told her father her name was written in Heaven; she then asked him to lead her so she could look up without watching the "ugly earth". At bedtime she asked Pauline every night: "Have I been good today? Are the little angels going to fly around me?" If the answer had not been yes she would have cried all night. Alone in the dark she was frightened; Pauline trained her out of it by sending her alone at night to fetch things from far rooms, "so that now I am really hard to frighten".
- When / where: c. 1878–1882, Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin; Pauline
- Source: Ms A 18r–18v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Pauline, est-ce que j'ai été bien mignonne aujourd'hui?... »
- Translation: "Pauline, have I been really good today?"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-026 — Pauline's strictness; "Pauline doesn't want me to"
- Claim: Thérèse wrote that Pauline never let a single imperfection pass, never scolded without reason, and never went back on a decision. Even her father had to follow Pauline's will: when he asked her to come for a walk, Thérèse answered "Pauline doesn't want me to," and he had to go and ask for her "pardon". When she was ill each winter, Pauline put her in her own bed; once she gave her a little knife with a mother-of-pearl star; Thérèse asked whether Pauline would sacrifice her watch to keep her from dying.
- When / where: c. 1878–1882, Les Buissonnets
- People: Pauline; Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 18v–19r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Pauline ne veut pas »
- Translation: "Pauline doesn't want me to."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: "Lorsque la petite Thérèse était malade, ce qui lui arrivait tous les hivers" — she was ill every winter as a child (health detail).
### F-OW-027 — The thimble and the glass: Pauline's answer about glory in Heaven
- Claim: Thérèse worried that not all the blessed would be equally happy. Pauline had her fill her father's big glass and her own tiny thimble with water and asked which was fuller; both were full. So in Heaven each receives as much glory as it can hold.
- When / where: c. 1880, Les Buissonnets
- People: Pauline
- Source: Ms A 19r–19v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « le grand «verre à Papa» ... mon tout petit dé »
- Translation: "Papa's big glass ... my tiny thimble"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-028 — The vision of the veiled man in the garden (father's illness foreseen)
- Claim: At about six or seven, alone at a garret window on a sunny afternoon, with her father away at Alençon, she saw a man dressed exactly like him, same height and walk but much more bent, his head covered with a kind of apron of an unclear colour, walking past her little garden towards the grove. She called "Papa, Papa!" in a trembling voice; he did not turn and vanished behind the trees. Marie, in the next room with Pauline, felt fear. Victoire denied hiding under her apron; the three searched and found nothing. Fourteen years later, talking with Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, both understood it as a picture of her father's illness: his face veiled like the Holy Face.
- When / where: c. 1879–1880, Les Buissonnets; understood c. 1893–1894 in the Carmel
- People: Marie; Pauline; Victoire; Louis Martin (absent)
- Source: Ms A 19v–21r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Sa tête était couverte d'une espèce de tablier de couleur indécise »
- Translation: "His head was covered with a kind of apron of no clear colour."
- Other sources: In the 1911 edited text (ch. II). See M-OW-01.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high that she reported it; the event itself is her account only.
- Notes: She calls it "une vision vraiment extraordinaire" and "la vision prophétique". Record also as a miracle-list entry (extraordinary event she reports).
### F-OW-029 — "If Papa falls, I'll die with him": the ladder
- Claim: Her father on a ladder called: "Get away, poor little one, if I fall I'll crush you." She felt an inner revolt and pressed herself against the ladder, thinking: "At least if Papa falls, I won't have the sorrow of seeing him die, since I'll die with him!" She told him that if he spoke to the men of the government they would make him King and France would be happy, but she was secretly glad no one knew him as she did, "he would no longer have been my King all to myself".
- When / where: c. 1880, Les Buissonnets
- People: Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 21r–21v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Éloigne-toi paup'tit, si je tombe je vais t'écraser. »
- Translation: "Move away, poor little one; if I fall I'll crush you."
- Other sources: The dialect-like "paup'tit" is absent from the 1911 text.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-030 — First sight of the sea at Trouville; the "golden furrow"
- Claim: At six or seven her father took them to Trouville; she could not stop looking at the sea. A lady and gentleman asked if she was his and called her pretty; she saw her father sign to them not to compliment her; "it was the first time I heard that I was pretty". At sunset she sat alone on a rock with Pauline, looked at the golden path of light on the water and resolved never to take her soul from Jesus' gaze.
- When / where: c. 1879–1880, Trouville
- People: Louis Martin; Pauline
- Source: Ms A 21v–22r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « C'était la première fois que j'entendais dire que j'étais gentille »
- Translation: "It was the first time I had heard anyone say I was pretty."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-031 — The abbey school: "the saddest years of my life"; bullied by an older girl
- Claim: At eight and a half she replaced Léonie as a day pupil at the Benedictine abbey. "The five years I spent there were the saddest of my life; if I had not had Céline with me I could not have stayed a single month without falling ill." Placed with older girls, she was often first; a girl of 13–14, "not very intelligent" but able to dominate pupils and even teachers, was jealous and "made me pay in a thousand ways for my little successes". She only cried and told no one, not even Pauline. At home she jumped on her father's knees and his kiss made her forget. For her first composition (Sacred History) she missed full marks by one point, not knowing the name of Moses' father.
- When / where: Oct 1881 onward, Abbaye Notre-Dame-du-Pré, Lisieux
- People: Céline; an unnamed older pupil; Louis Martin; Pauline
- Source: Ms A 22r–22v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « les cinq années que j'y passai furent les plus tristes de ma vie »
- Translation: "the five years I spent there were the saddest of my life"
- New to English readers: partial (Wikipedia mentions bullying; not her words)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-032 — She could not play; games of "hermits" with cousin Marie; walking with eyes shut
- Claim: On Thursday holidays she had to play with her cousins and the Maudelonde girls and dance quadrilles, which bored her; "not knowing how to play like other children, I was not a pleasant companion". With cousin Marie Guérin she invented a game of two hermits; in the street they said the rosary on their fingers, but once "the younger hermit" made a big sign of the cross over a cake and "all the worldly people" laughed. Another time both walked home with eyes shut, fell over crates outside a shop, and the angry shopkeeper came out; Jeanne then separated them.
- When / where: c. 1882–1884, Lisieux
- People: Marie Guérin; Jeanne Guérin; Céline; the Maudelonde girls
- Source: Ms A 23r–23v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « les deux petites étourdies tombèrent ensemble sur des caisses »
- Translation: "the two little scatterbrains fell together over some crates"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-033 — Céline's doll "hung from her nose"
- Claim: When angry, Céline told her "You're not my little girl any more"; Thérèse then cried until forgiven. To comfort her Céline made a doll kiss "its aunt"; once the doll hugged her so hard its two little arms went up her nose, and Thérèse laughed with all her heart. At New Year each had 10 sous to buy 5 or 6 presents for the other at the bazaar.
- When / where: c. 1882–1884, Les Buissonnets
- People: Céline
- Source: Ms A 24r–24v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « elle me passa ses deux petits bras dans le nez »
- Translation: "it stuck both its little arms up my nose"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-034 — Learning by chance that Pauline would enter Carmel
- Claim: Pauline had once said she would wait for Thérèse to be old enough to go with her to a distant desert. Thérèse overheard Pauline telling Marie about her coming entry to Carmel. "In an instant I understood what life was... it was only suffering and a continual separation." Hearing it by surprise "was as if a sword had been driven into my heart". Pauline then explained Carmel, and Thérèse felt with certainty that Carmel was "the desert where God wanted me also to hide", "not for Pauline but for Jesus alone".
- When / where: summer 1882, Les Buissonnets
- People: Pauline; Marie
- Source: Ms A 25v–26r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « ce fut comme si un glaive s'était enfoncé dans mon coeur »
- Translation: "it was as if a sword had been plunged into my heart"
- New to English readers: partial (baseline says she was hurt by Pauline's departure)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-035 — Age nine: she asks the prioress to be received; told to wait until 16
- Claim: Pauline took her to see the prioress. To speak alone with her, Thérèse told cousin Marie that they should each "confide secrets" to the Mother one at a time. Mère Marie de Gonzague believed in her vocation but said they did not receive postulants of nine; she must wait until 16. Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin kept saying she was pretty; Thérèse told God she wanted to be a Carmelite for Him alone, not for praise.
- When / where: 1882, parlour of the Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Pauline; Marie Guérin; Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin
- Source: Ms A 26r–26v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « on ne recevait pas de postulantes de 9 ans et qu'il faudrait attendre mes 16 ans »
- Translation: "they did not take postulants of nine; I would have to wait until I was sixteen"
- New to English readers: yes (not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-036 — Pauline's entry (2 Oct 1882) and the Thursday parlours
- Claim: On 2 Oct 1882 she received Pauline's last kiss; her aunt took the girls to Mass while her father went to the Carmel; the family wept in church and people stared. The same day school restarted. On Thursdays the family visited the parlour; Thérèse got two or three minutes at the end and spent them crying. She did not understand that Pauline spoke to Jeanne and Marie Guérin out of courtesy to her aunt; she said in her heart "Pauline is lost to me!!!" "My mind developed so much in suffering that I soon fell ill."
- When / where: 2 Oct 1882 onward, Lisieux
- People: Pauline; Louis Martin; Mme Guérin; Jeanne and Marie Guérin
- Source: Ms A 26v–27r; 41r–41v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Pauline est perdue pour moi!!! »
- Translation: "Pauline is lost to me!!!"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-037 — How she chose the name "Thérèse of the Child Jesus"
- Claim: Before her first visit to Pauline in Carmel she wondered in bed what her name would be; there was already a Sr Thérèse de Jésus, but "my beautiful name of Thérèse could not be taken from me"; she thought "How happy I would be to be called Thérèse of the Child Jesus!" She said nothing, but in the parlour Mère Marie de Gonzague, asking what name to give her, chose that very name.
- When / where: Oct 1882, Carmel parlour
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Source: Ms A 31r–31v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Oh! que je serais heureuse de m'appeler Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus! »
- Translation: "Oh, how happy I would be to be called Thérèse of the Child Jesus!"
- New to English readers: yes (baseline gives the name, not the story)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-038 — The 1883 illness: symptoms as she describes them
- Claim: From late 1882 she had a constant headache, not very painful. At Easter 1883, while her father was in Paris with Marie and Léonie, she stayed at the Guérins'. After her uncle spoke to her of her mother and she cried, she was seized that evening, undressing, by a "strange trembling" that lasted almost all night despite blankets and hot bottles. Dr Notta judged it a very grave illness "which no child so young had ever had". Her father thought "his little girl would go mad or die". She seemed in delirium, said things without sense, often seemed to faint and could not move, "I would have let them do anything, even kill me", yet she says she heard and remembers everything and never lost her reason. Once she could not open her eyes for a long time. She had great terrors of simple remedies. She would not let Marie leave her and called her "Mama" constantly. She disliked visitors "sitting round my bed in a row like onions and looking at me like a strange animal".
- When / where: 25 Mar – 13 May 1883, Guérin house then Les Buissonnets, Lisieux
- People: Isidore and Céline Guérin; Dr Notta; Marie; Léonie; Céline; Victoire; Louis Martin; the maid Aimée (at the Guérins)
- Source: Ms A 27r–29v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « sa petite fille allait devenir folle ou bien qu'elle allait mourir » ; « en rang d'oignons et me regardant comme une bête curieuse »
- Translation: "his little girl was going to go mad or else die"; "in a row like onions, staring at me like a strange animal"
- Other sources: The 1911 edited text removes "folle" (mad) and the "rang d'oignons" line; it keeps "résignation admirable de mon père" (ch. III). Compare other stage-2 parts (Marie's testimony; Dr Notta).
- New to English readers: yes (the symptoms, the "mad" fear and the onion line were cut from the edited text)
- Confidence: high as her report; the diagnosis is not stated beyond "très grave".
- Notes: She attributes the illness to the devil "furious at your entry into Carmel". She does not name the disease. Medical interpretation belongs to other sources.
### F-OW-039 — Brief respite for Pauline's clothing (6 Apr 1883)
- Claim: Though very ill she kept saying she would be well enough to see Pauline take the habit. She was able to go, sit on Pauline's knees and caress her; then the carriage took her home, she was put to bed insisting she was cured, and the next day the illness returned worse.
- When / where: 6 Apr 1883, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Pauline (Sr Agnès de Jésus)
- Source: Ms A 28r–28v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « j'assurais être parfaitement guérie »
- Translation: "I insisted I was completely cured."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high. The date 6 Apr 1883 is from other sources, not from her text.
### F-OW-040 — The smile of the Virgin (13 May 1883)
- Claim: Her father gave Marie gold coins to have Masses said at Notre-Dame des Victoires in Paris. On a Sunday during the novena of Masses, Marie went into the garden, leaving Thérèse with Léonie reading by the window; Thérèse called "Mama... Mama..." more and more loudly; Marie came back but Thérèse could not show she recognised her. Marie, Léonie and Céline knelt by the bed and turned to the statue of the Virgin (the one that "had spoken twice to Mama"). Thérèse too prayed; "suddenly the Blessed Virgin seemed to me beautiful, so beautiful that I had never seen anything so beautiful... what went to the depths of my soul was the 'ravishing smile of the Blessed Virgin'." Two big tears ran silently down her cheeks. She looked down and saw Marie looking at her, who thought "Thérèse is cured!" The healing worked "not all at once, but gently".
- When / where: Sunday 13 May 1883 (Pentecost), Marie's room, Les Buissonnets
- People: Marie; Léonie; Céline; Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 29v–30v; date from Ms A 86r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Tout à coup la Sainte Vierge me parut belle, si belle que jamais je n'avais rien vu de si beau »
- Translation: "All at once the Blessed Virgin looked beautiful to me, so beautiful that I had never seen anything so beautiful."
- Other sources: See M-OW-02. Marie's and Léonie's testimony belong to other stage-2 parts.
- New to English readers: no (in baseline), but the details (the calling "Mama", the novena of Masses, the gold coins) are partial.
- Confidence: high as her account.
- Notes: She does not say the statue moved; she says the Virgin "seemed" beautiful and smiled.
### F-OW-041 — Four years of torment after she told the secret of the smile
- Claim: She had resolved to tell no one, "for then my happiness would disappear". Marie guessed and questioned her; she told her. Marie asked to tell the Carmel. In the parlour the nuns asked whether the Virgin carried the Child Jesus, whether there was much light, etc.; she could only say the Virgin seemed very beautiful and smiled at her. Seeing they imagined something else, "I imagined I had lied". For four years the memory was "a real pain of soul"; she could not look at herself "without a feeling of deep horror". Peace came back only at Notre-Dame des Victoires in Paris in Nov 1887.
- When / where: May 1883 – Nov 1887
- People: Marie; Pauline; Mère Marie de Gonzague; other nuns
- Source: Ms A 30v–31r; 56v–57r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je me figurai avoir menti »
- Translation: "I imagined I had lied."
- New to English readers: yes (not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-042 — Scruple that she had faked her illness
- Claim: "Long after my cure I believed I had been ill on purpose, and that was a real martyrdom for my soul." Marie and her confessor reassured her; the doubt lasted until she entered Carmel, when "the Father of our souls" (Fr Pichon, 1888) took it away "as with his hand".
- When / where: 1883–1888
- People: Marie; her confessor; Fr Almire Pichon SJ
- Source: Ms A 28v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « j'ai cru que j'avais fait exprès d'être malade et ce fut là un vrai martyre pour mon âme »
- Translation: "I believed I had made myself ill on purpose, and that was a real martyrdom for my soul."
- Other sources: Absent from the 1911 edited text ("fait exprès d'être malade").
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-043 — First "entry into the world" at Alençon, summer 1883
- Claim: Three months after her cure her father took the family to Alençon; she prayed at her mother's grave. For two weeks she was "feted, spoiled, admired"; she admits "this life had charms for me". She judged their Alençon friends "too worldly"; many of those young, rich people she saw in châteaux and parks had since died.
- When / where: Aug 1883, Alençon
- People: Louis Martin; family friends (unnamed)
- Source: Ms A 32r–32v

- Rights: public-domain

- Translation: "I admit that this life had its charms for me."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-044 — Joan of Arc and the desire to be "a great saint"
- Claim: Reading of French heroines, especially "the Venerable Joan of Arc", she wanted to imitate them and "thought I was born for glory"; God made her understand her glory would not be seen by mortal eyes: it would be "to become a great saint!!!" She writes that she still has "the same audacious confidence" after seven years in religion, relying not on her merits but on God.
- When / where: c. 1883–1884; reflection 1895
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 31v–32r

- New to English readers: partial

### F-OW-045 — "I think": prayer behind the bed curtain
- Claim: A teacher at the abbey asked what she did on holidays when alone. She said she went into a space behind her bed, closed it with the curtain, and "thought". "About what?" "About God, about life... about eternity, I just think!" The nun laughed and later often asked if she was still "thinking". Thérèse: "I understand now that I was praying without knowing it."

- People: an unnamed Benedictine teacher

### F-OW-046 — First Communion, 8 May 1884: "a fusion"
- Claim: She prepared for three months with a little book Pauline made (flowers for acts of virtue, buds for acts of love) and Marie's evening talks. During the boarding retreat she could not do her own hair; the dormitory mistress laughed at "a big girl of 11 not knowing how to manage". She wore a big crucifix from Léonie in her belt "like the missionaries". On the day: "it was a kiss of love, I felt loved... it was no longer a look but a fusion, they were no longer two, Thérèse had disappeared, like a drop of water lost in the ocean." She cried; companions thought it was for her dead mother or her sister in Carmel; "they did not understand that all the joy of Heaven had come into one heart." In the afternoon she read the act of consecration to the Virgin for all the children. That evening she saw Pauline, who had made profession the same day, with a white veil and crown of roses. Her father gave her a watch.
- When / where: 8 May 1884, abbey chapel, Lisieux
- People: Pauline; Marie; Léonie; Céline; Louis Martin; Abbé Domin (retreat preacher)
- Source: Ms A 33r–35v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « ce n'était plus un regard mais une fusion, ils n'étaient plus deux »
- Translation: "it was no longer a look but a fusion; they were no longer two"
- New to English readers: partial (date is in baseline; her words are not)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-047 — Desire for suffering after her second Communion
- Claim: At her second Communion (Ascension 1884) she repeated St Paul, "It is no longer I who live". Marie once told her she would probably not walk the way of suffering; the next day after Communion she felt "a great desire for suffering" and the certainty that Jesus had many crosses for her. "Suffering became my attraction." She often repeated the Imitation's prayer: "O Jesus, unspeakable sweetness, change for me into bitterness all the consolations of the earth!"
- When / where: May 1884, Lisieux
- People: Marie
- Source: Ms A 36r–36v

- Translation: "Suffering became what drew me."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-048 — Confirmation, 14 Jun 1884: "a light breeze", Léonie in tears
- Claim: Because the bishop was delayed she had two days of retreat; the class picked big daisies at Mont-Cassin for Corpus Christi. At confirmation she felt not "a violent wind" but "the light breeze" Elijah heard; "on that day I received the strength to suffer". Léonie was her sponsor and cried through the whole ceremony.

- People: Léonie; Mgr Hugonin (bishop, not named here)
- Source: Ms A 36v–37r

- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-049 — At school: the bird cemetery and the storyteller silenced
- Claim: At recreation she often leaned against a tree and watched. She invented a game of burying dead birds found under the trees; other pupils helped and the "cemetery" had trees and flowers sized for "our little feathered ones". She told invented serial stories that lasted days; older girls joined the listeners, until the mistress forbade "my trade of orator", wanting them to run and not "discourse". Abbé Domin called her "his little doctor" because of her name; she wept if she lost first place by one word of catechism. Her uncle's family thought her "a little ignoramus, good and gentle... but incapable and clumsy"; she blamed her "cat's handwriting" and "spelling that is anything but natural".
- When / where: 1884–1886, abbey school, Lisieux
- People: Abbé Domin; school companions; Guérin family
- Source: Ms A 37r–37v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « mon écriture de chat et mon orthographe qui n'est rien moins que naturelle »
- Translation: "my cat-scratch handwriting and my spelling, which is anything but natural"
- Other sources: The 1911 edited text drops "écriture de chat".
- New to English readers: partial (cemetery and stories); yes (handwriting/spelling self-mockery)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-050 — A friend's cold look; the ring she kept
- Claim: She tried to make friends with two girls. One left school for some months; Thérèse kept a little ring she had given her; on her return she got "only an indifferent look". "I did not beg for an affection that was refused me", but she kept praying for her "and I love her still". She tried to win a teacher's favour as Céline did, and failed.
- When / where: c. 1884–1885, abbey school
- People: two unnamed schoolfriends
- Source: Ms A 38r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je n'obtins qu'un regard indifférent »
- Translation: "I got only an indifferent look."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-051 — Scruples for a year and a half (1885–1886)
- Claim: During the retreat before her "second Communion" (May 1885) she was seized by "the terrible illness of scruples"; it lasted a year and a half. Every thought and simple action troubled her; she found rest only by telling Marie, and felt obliged to tell even "the extravagant thoughts I had about her". Marie curled her hair every evening (to please Papa) while Thérèse cried and recounted her scruples. She was so obedient that her confessor never knew: she confessed only the number of sins Marie allowed, "not one more".
- When / where: May 1885 – late 1886, Les Buissonnets
- People: Marie
- Source: Ms A 39r–39v; 41v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « jamais mon confesseur n'a connu ma vilaine maladie »
- Translation: "my confessor never knew about my nasty illness"
- New to English readers: yes (baseline does not mention scruples)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-052 — Trouville 1885: blue ribbons confessed as a sin; the faked headache
- Claim: During her father's journey to Constantinople (1885), Marie sent Céline and Thérèse to their aunt at Trouville for 15 days: donkey rides, fishing for sand-eels. At 12½ she was delighted with sky-blue ribbons for her hair, then confessed this pleasure as if it were a sin. One evening she imitated her cousin Marie, who whimpered about headaches and was petted: Thérèse sat whimpering in a corner saying "I have a headache". No one believed a headache could make her cry; Jeanne thought she had a scruple. She was "cured for life of the desire to attract attention" and thought of La Fontaine's fable of the donkey and the little dog. She adds that she had a headache "almost every day" and did not complain.
- When / where: summer 1885, Trouville
- People: Mme Guérin; Jeanne and Marie Guérin; Céline; Louis Martin (abroad)
- Source: Ms A 41v–42r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Moi qui presque chaque jour avais aussi mal à la tête et ne m'en plaignais pas »
- Translation: "I, who had a headache almost every day too and didn't complain"
- Other sources: The edited text keeps the donkey fable (ch. IV).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-053 — Lessons with "Mme Papinau" and her cat
- Claim: At 13 she left the abbey (she fell ill when Céline finished school) and took lessons at the home of "Mme Papinau", "a very good, very learned person with a bit of an old maid's manner", who lived with her mother and a cat that purred on Thérèse's exercise books. The old mother, Mme Cochain, announced her in a slow voice: "Mme Pâpinau... Ma...d'môizelle Thê...rèse est là!..." Visitors talked during lessons; she heard a lady say she had lovely hair and another ask who "this very pretty girl" was, and felt the pleasure of vanity.
- When / where: 1886–1887, Lisieux
- People: Mme Papinau (Valentine Papineau, name spelled "Papinau" by Thérèse); her mother Mme Cochain
- Source: Ms A 39v–40r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je devais supporter qu'elle fasse son ronron sur mes cahiers »
- Translation: "I had to put up with it purring on my exercise books"
- Other sources: The 1911 text keeps the compliments but removes the names and the cat.
- New to English readers: yes (names, cat, mimicry)
- Confidence: high for her text; "Valentine Papineau" is the modern spelling (verify in other parts).
### F-OW-054 — Children of Mary: going back to the abbey alone
- Claim: Having left school early, she was not entitled to join the Children of Mary as a former pupil. Not wanting to be less a child of the Virgin than her sisters, she asked humbly; the mistress required her to come two afternoons a week. She had no teacher-friend there, worked in silence, and then went up to the chapel gallery before the Blessed Sacrament until her father came. "It was better to speak to God than to speak about God."
- When / where: 1886, abbey, Lisieux
- People: the first mistress; Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 40v–41r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « il valait mieux parler à Dieu que de parler de Dieu »
- Translation: "it was better to talk to God than to talk about God"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-055 — Her attic room: "a real bazaar"
- Claim: After leaving school she took Pauline's old painting room and filled it: a big black wooden cross without a figure; a hamper with muslin and pink ribbons; Pauline's portrait at ten; a large cage of birds "whose song gave visitors a headache"; a white cabinet with books and a statue of the Virgin with fresh flowers; saints' statues, shell baskets, cardboard boxes; potted plants hanging outside the window; a table with a green cloth holding an hourglass, a statue of St Joseph, a watch-stand, an inkwell; some lame chairs and Pauline's doll bed. She compares it to Xavier de Maistre's "Journey Around My Room".
- When / where: 1886–1888, Les Buissonnets
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 42v–43r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « C'était un vrai bazar, un assemblage de piété et de curiosités, un jardin et une volière »
- Translation: "It was a real bazaar, a mix of piety and curiosities, a garden and an aviary."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-056 — Crying over forgotten cornflowers; Léonie's two months with the Poor Clares
- Claim: A month before Marie's entry, her father took them to Alençon; Thérèse cried at her mother's grave because she had forgotten the cornflowers she had picked for her. "I made myself unhappy over everything!" During the trip Léonie suddenly entered the Poor Clares; their father, embarrassed, told them she already had the habit; "like us he found it very odd". Léonie told them to look at her eyes because they would not see them again; she came back after two months.
- When / where: Jul–Aug 1886, Alençon
- People: Louis Martin; Léonie; Marie; Céline
- Source: Ms A 43r–43v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « il trouvait cela bien drôle, mais ne voulait rien dire, voyant combien Marie était mécontente »
- Translation: "he found it very odd but would say nothing, seeing how displeased Marie was"
- New to English readers: yes (Léonie's Poor Clare attempt is not in the baseline)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-057 — Marie's entry (15 Oct 1886); prayers to her four dead siblings
- Claim: After learning of Marie's decision, Thérèse knocked at her door every time she passed "to stock up on kisses". Marie entered Carmel on 15 Oct 1886; only Céline and Thérèse were left at home. Still scrupulous and without a confidante, she prayed to the four little brothers and sisters who had died young; peace came, "and I understood that if I was loved on earth I was also loved in Heaven".
- When / where: 15 Oct 1886 and after, Lisieux
- People: Marie; Céline; the four dead Martin children
- Source: Ms A 43r–44r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je voulais faire provision de baisers »
- Translation: "I wanted to lay in a stock of kisses"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-058 — "I cried for having cried": her oversensitivity before Christmas 1886
- Claim: As the youngest she was not used to doing housework; after Marie left she sometimes made the bed or brought in Céline's flower pots "to please God", but if Céline did not look surprised and grateful she cried. "I was really unbearable because of my excessive sensitivity"; if she hurt someone she cried, and when she began to be consoled "I cried for having cried". Someone had told her: "You cry so much in childhood that later you'll have no tears left."
- When / where: autumn 1886, Les Buissonnets
- People: Céline
- Source: Ms A 44v–45r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je pleurais d'avoir pleuré »
- Translation: "I cried for having cried."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-059 — Christmas night 1886: the shoes in the fireplace
- Claim: Coming home from midnight Mass (where she received Communion) she looked forward to her shoes in the fireplace, a childhood custom Céline kept up. Her father, tired from midnight Mass, was annoyed to see the shoes and said words that "pierced my heart": "Well, thank goodness it's the last year!" Going upstairs to take off her hat, with tears in her eyes, Céline said: "Oh Thérèse, don't go down, it would hurt you too much to look in your shoes right away." But "Jesus had changed her heart": she held back her tears, went down, took the shoes, set them before her father and joyfully pulled out the gifts, "looking as happy as a queen". Papa laughed; Céline thought she was dreaming. She calls it "the grace of my complete conversion"; "the work I had not been able to do in ten years, Jesus did in an instant".
- When / where: night of 24–25 Dec 1886, Les Buissonnets
- People: Louis Martin; Céline
- Source: Ms A 44v–45v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Enfin, heureusement que c'est la dernière année!... »
- Translation: "Well, thank goodness it's the last year!"
- Other sources: The 1911 edited text changes her father's words to a gentler sentence: « Pour une grande fille comme Thérèse, c'est là une surprise trop enfantine; je l'espère, ce sera la dernière année. » ("For a big girl like Thérèse this surprise is too childish; I hope it will be the last year"), HA 1911 ch. V. Céline's words are also changed. See card on Pauline's edits.
- New to English readers: partial (the event is in baseline; the unedited words of Louis are not in Taylor 1912)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: The manuscript wording shows her father irritated, not gently reasoning. Valuable for a truthful portrait.
### F-OW-335 — Her first letters: Pauline held her on her lap and guided her hand
- Claim: At age four Thérèse "wrote" her first letters with help. Pauline held her on her lap because she could not yet hold a pen-holder. In the next letter she says Marie will no longer guide her hand and she cannot write alone. In LT 1 she calls herself "a little imp who always laughs" and says she spends the day teasing her poor little sisters.
- When / where: 4 April 1877 and 12–17 April 1877, Alençon (the family still lived there; her mother died in August 1877).
- People: Louise Magdelaine (recipient, a girl she did not know); Pauline Martin (sister); Marie Martin (sister); "ma soeur Marie Aloysia" and "ma soeur Louise de Gonzague" of the Visitation; Jeanne and Marie Guérin (cousins).
- Source: LT 1 (to Louise Magdelaine, 4 Apr 1877), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-1-a-louise-magdelaine-4-avril-1877/ ; LT 2 (to Jeanne and Marie Guérin, 12–17 Apr 1877), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-2-a-jeanne-et-marie-guerin-12-17-avril-1877/
- Source type: own-writing (dictated / hand guided)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « elle me tient sur ses genoux car je ne sais pas seulement tenir un porte-plume, elle veut que je vous dise que je suis une petite paresseuse, mais ce n'est pas vrai car je travaille toute la journée à faire des malices à mes pauvres petites soeurs enfin je suis un petit lutin qui rit toujours » ; « Marie ne veut plus me conduire la main et je ne sais pas écrire toute seule »
- Translation: "She is holding me on her knees because I don't even know how to hold a pen-holder. She wants me to tell you I am a little lazybones, but that is not true, because I work all day long at playing tricks on my poor little sisters. So I am a little imp who always laughs." / "Marie will not guide my hand any more and I do not know how to write by myself."
- Other sources: Ms A describes her as a lively, stubborn small child (baseline does not cover this letter).
- New to English readers: yes — "petit lutin qui rit toujours" is ABSENT from the 1911 edition; not in baseline.
- Confidence: high — exact text; note that the words were probably Pauline's pen with the child's dictation.
- Notes: The letter itself says Pauline told her what to say ("elle veut que je vous dise"). So this is a child's voice shaped by an older sister. She adds that she "knows no other people" than the two Visitation nuns.
### F-OW-336 — Age five: a bead ring from a shop lady at her aunt's
- Claim: While staying with her aunt Guérin in Lisieux, Thérèse went to buy grey stockings with her aunt. The shop lady gave her beads and she made herself a ring. Her cousin Marie was in the country, and she played alone with her aunt.
- When / where: 26 June 1878, Lisieux (Guérin home).
- People: Pauline Martin (recipient); Céline Fournet Guérin, "ma tante"; Marie Guérin (cousin).
- Source: LT 5 (to Pauline, 26 Jun 1878), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-5-a-pauline-26-juin-1878/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « J'ai été acheter des bas gris avec ma tante et la dame m'a donné des perles. Je me suis fait une bague avec. »
- Translation: "I went to buy grey stockings with my aunt and the lady gave me some beads. I made myself a ring with them."
- New to English readers: yes — small childhood detail, not in baseline or 1911.
- Confidence: high — exact text. The spelling here is clean, so an adult may have corrected it (not stated).
- Notes: None.
### F-OW-337 — Age seven: her own spelling, and she knows it is bad
- Claim: In a letter to Pauline written at her aunt's house, Thérèse admits she makes many mistakes and is "hardly skilful". The letter keeps her child spelling ("técrire", "baucoup", "conais", "gerre abile", "segon cienc", "himages"). She reports four good-marks (bons-points) the first day and five the second. While Marie does the accounts, she amuses herself hanging up pretty pictures her aunt gave her.
- When / where: 1 December 1880, Lisieux, at the Guérin house ("nous sommes chez ma Tante").
- People: Pauline (recipient); Marie (sister, doing the accounts); Mme Guérin (aunt); Louis Martin ("Papa").
- Source: LT 6 (to Pauline, 1 Dec 1880), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-6-a-pauline-1er-decembre-1880/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je fais baucoup de fautes, mais tu conais bien ta petite Thérèse et tu sait bien que Je ne suis gerre abile. [...] J'ai eu quatre bons-points le prmier Jours et le segon cienc. »
- Translation: "I make lots of mistakes, but you know your little Thérèse well, and you know I am not very clever at it. [...] I got four good-marks the first day and the second five."
- New to English readers: yes — the misspelled original is not in the 1911 edition or baseline.
- Confidence: high — the Carmel transcription keeps her spelling.
- Notes: Good evidence for her real childhood writing level at nearly eight. Compare the tidy spelling of LT 5 (1878), which may have been corrected by an adult.
### F-OW-338 — Age eight: roses thrown at St Domitia, and "show my letter to no one"
- Claim: Thérèse writes to Pauline, who is on holiday at Houlgate. She asks if Pauline rode a donkey. She thanks Pauline for giving her a holiday from lessons while Pauline is away. On St Domitia's day her aunt put a pink sash on her and she threw roses to St Domitia. She ends: "Show my letter to no one."
- When / where: about 4 July 1881; Thérèse in Lisieux, Pauline at Houlgate.
- People: Pauline (recipient, her teacher at home); Mme Guérin (aunt); Marie; Marie-Thérèse and "la petite Marguerite" (people with Pauline, not identified in the letter).
- Source: LT 7 (to Pauline, 4 Jul 1881), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-7-a-pauline-4-juillet-1881/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je te remercie bien de me donner congé pendant que Tu est à houlgat. [...] Si Tu savais le Jour de Sainte Domitia ma Tante ma mis une sinture rose et j'ai Jeté des roses à Sainte domitia. Ne montre ma lettre à personne. »
- Translation: "Thank you for giving me a holiday while you are at Houlgate. [...] If you only knew: on St Domitia's day my aunt put a pink sash on me and I threw roses to St Domitia. Don't show my letter to anyone."
- New to English readers: yes — absent from 1911; not in baseline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Shows that Pauline was her teacher ("congé" = time off from lessons). Spelling still childish ("sinture", "houlgat").
### F-OW-339 — Age nine: her "confession" to Mère Marie de Gonzague — she talks back
- Claim: Writing to the Carmel prioress, Thérèse confesses that lately she always answers back when Marie tells her to do something. She repeats a family saying: when Pauline was small and made excuses to her aunt at Le Mans, the aunt said "As many holes, as many pegs". Thérèse says she is "even worse". She wants to put a pretty flower in each "hole" and offer it to the Child Jesus to prepare for First Communion. She signs "Thérésita".
- When / where: November–December 1882 (date uncertain in the source), Lisieux. Pauline had entered the Carmel in October 1882.
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress of the Lisieux Carmel); Marie Martin (sister, in charge of her at home); Pauline; "ma tante du Mans" (Sr Marie-Dosithée, Visitation nun of Le Mans — the name is not in the letter).
- Source: LT 9 (to Mère Marie de Gonzague, Nov–Dec 1882), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-9-a-mere-marie-de-gonzague-nov-dec-1882/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Depuis quelque temps je répons toujours quand Marie me dit de faire quelquechose [...] elle lui disait : Autant de trous autant de chevilles, mais moi c'est bien pire encore. »
- Translation: "For some time now I always answer back when Marie tells me to do something. [...] she used to tell her: 'As many holes, as many pegs.' But with me it is even worse."
- New to English readers: yes — "autant de chevilles" ABSENT from 1911; not in baseline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: A child's admitted fault, in her own words. "Autant de trous autant de chevilles" means: for every fault, an excuse ready. The identification of the Le Mans aunt is from general family history, not from this letter.
### F-OW-340 — Preparing for First Communion: the little book of "practices" and the rosary of practices
- Claim: Pauline sent Thérèse a little handmade book for her First Communion preparation. Thérèse loved its picture of a dove giving its heart to the Child Jesus. She tries each day to do as many "practices" (small acts of virtue) as she can and says the short prayers "that make the scent of roses". She thanks Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin for a "chapelet de pratique" (a counting rosary for good acts) and for embroidering the cover of her book. She had not written earlier because she thought letters were not allowed in Lent.
- When / where: 1–6 March 1884, Lisieux.
- People: Pauline (Sr Agnès de Jésus, Carmelite); Marie (showed her the book); Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin (Carmelite of Lisieux); Mère Marie de Gonzague; Léonie and Céline.
- Source: LT 11 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 1–6 Mar 1884), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-11-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-1-6-mars-1884/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Tous les jours je tâche de faire le plus de pratiques que je peux [...] Remercie bien pour moi ma Sr Thérèse de St Augustin de son joli petit chapelet de pratique, et de m'avoir brodé la belle couverture de mon livre. »
- Translation: "Every day I try to do as many practices as I can [...] Please thank Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin for me for her pretty little rosary of practices, and for embroidering the lovely cover of my book."
- Other sources: Ms A mentions the book and the counting of sacrifices (partly in baseline as "preparation for First Communion").
- New to English readers: partial — the book is described in Ms A (Taylor 1912); the letter's details (embroidered cover, the nun's name) are not in baseline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin appears here as a kind helper in 1884. The letter says nothing about any later relationship.
### F-OW-341 — First and second Communion keepsakes
- Claim: For her First Communion (8 May 1884) Thérèse gave small written keepsakes to Marie and Céline. On 21 May 1885 she gave Marie a keepsake "of the second Communion of your little daughter". She calls herself Marie's "little daughter".
- When / where: 8 May 1884 and 21 May 1885, Lisieux.
- People: Marie Martin (godmother and "mother" after Zélie's death); Céline; Marie Guérin (undated keepsake LT 14).

- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « A ma chère petite Marie souvenir de la première communion de / Ta petite fille Thérèse »
- Translation: "To my dear little Marie, a keepsake of the first communion of your little daughter Thérèse."
- New to English readers: partial — First Communion is baseline; the keepsakes are not.
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-342 — May 1885: constant headaches before the second Communion retreat
- Claim: Her aunt asked for news of her health. Thérèse writes that she is better than on Sunday, but "I still have a very bad headache". She will enter retreat on Sunday evening; the Communion stays fixed on the 21st. She signs "enf[ant] des Sts Anges" (a member of the Holy Angels association at school).
- When / where: 10–17 May 1885, Lisieux.
- People: Mme Céline Guérin (aunt); Jeanne and Marie Guérin (Marie "finishing her cure").
- Source: LT 16 (to Mme Guérin, fragments, 10–17 May 1885), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-16-a-mme-guerin-fragments-10-17-mai-1885/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je vais mieux que dimanche, cependant j'ai toujours très mal à la tête. »
- Translation: "I am better than on Sunday, but I still have a very bad headache."
- Other sources: Ms A says her scruples began in the retreat before this second Communion (1885).
- New to English readers: yes — the headache report is not in baseline or 1911.
- Confidence: high for the words; the letter is fragmentary.
- Notes: A health detail between the 1883 illness and the 1885–86 scruples. The letter does not give a cause.
### F-OW-343 — The "little Queen" and her "Papa-King": Pauline's verses copied for her father's feast
- Claim: Her father was away on a journey on his feast day (St Louis, 25 August). Pauline had written verses for Thérèse to recite to him. Since she could not recite them, Thérèse copied them into her letter: "Le Souhaits d'une petite Reine pour la fête de son Papa-Roi" (sixteen quatrains). She signs "Ta Reine".
- When / where: 25 August 1885, Lisieux; Louis Martin travelling.
- People: Louis Martin (father); Pauline (author of the verses).
- Source: LT 18 (to M. Martin, 25 Aug 1885), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-18-a-m-martin-25-aout-1885/

- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Mais je n'ai point d'aile qui brille / Je ne suis point un Séraphin / Je suis une petite fille / Qu'on tient encore par la main. »
- Translation: "But I have no shining wing, I am not a Seraph, I am a little girl who is still held by the hand."
- Other sources: 1911 (Pauline's biographical chapter) says her father called her "sa petite reine... de France et de Navarre".
- New to English readers: yes — the poem and "Papa-Roi" are ABSENT from 1911; the "little queen" nickname is partial.
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-344 — Private lessons with Madame Papinot, and a white dress for the procession
- Claim: After leaving the Benedictine school, Thérèse had lessons with Madame Papinot. The lessons "go very well" and have been increased, so she had no time to write. Tomorrow she will be "in white for the procession"; Marie tried her clothes on her. She apologizes for writing without a draft ("brouillon").
- When / where: Saturday 26 June 1886, "Aux Buissonnets", Lisieux.
- People: Marie Guérin (cousin); Madame Papinot (private teacher); Marie Martin; Céline; Pauline.
- Source: LT 19 (to Marie Guérin, 26 Jun 1886), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-19-a-marie-guerin-26-juin-1886/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « pour les leçons elles marchent toujours très bien, elles sont augmentées depuis quelques temps [...] demain je serai en blanc pour la procession Marie m'a esséyé mes affaires »
- Translation: "The lessons are still going very well; they have been increased for some time now [...] tomorrow I will be in white for the procession; Marie tried my things on me."
- New to English readers: yes — "Papinot" ABSENT from 1911; not in baseline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She normally made a draft before writing a letter ("je n'ai pas eu le temps de faire un brouillon").
### F-OW-345 — The swing and the rings: Marie fears she will become hunchbacked
- Claim: Marie was afraid Thérèse would become hunchbacked, so she asked Papa to hang rings and a swing. Thérèse prefers the swing; her hands are all red from the rings. The day before she spent the afternoon at Madame Maudelonde's with Céline and Hélène. Madame Papinot gave her a day off for the feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel so she could attend the sermon.
- When / where: Thursday 15 July 1886, Les Buissonnets.
- People: Marie Guérin (recipient); Marie Martin; Louis Martin; Mme Maudelonde (a relative of the Guérins); Céline Maudelonde and Hélène Maudelonde (the letter says "Céline et Hélène"); Mme Papinot.

- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je viens de me balancer ; Marie a peur que je devienne bossue et elle a demendé à Papa de pendre les anneaux et la balançoire ; les anneaux me plaisent moins que la balançoire, j'ai les mains toutes rouges d'y avoir été. »
- Translation: "I have just been on the swing. Marie is afraid I will become hunchbacked, and she asked Papa to hang up the rings and the swing. I like the rings less than the swing; my hands are all red from them."

- Notes: A concrete body/health detail at age 13: the family worried about her posture.
### F-OW-346 — Waiting for Marie's return: candles to the Virgin, a letter from "Monsieur Pichon", and the maid Félicité
- Claim: Marie was travelling with their father. The family worried that Marie would not know to meet "le Père" at Dover on his return. Céline sent letters to Dover and Calais poste restante. Thérèse says the Virgin "had a candle every day" and she prayed hard. A telegram arrived and relieved them. A letter from "Monsieur Pichon" to Papa was opened on Pauline's advice. Thérèse begs Marie not to bring her any souvenir. In a postscript: "Félicité ... has been in a charming mood since you left."
- When / where: Saturday 2 October 1886, 6 p.m., feast of the Holy Angels, Lisieux.
- People: Marie Martin (godmother, "Marraine"); Louis Martin; "le Père" (not named; a priest returning via Dover); "Monsieur Pichon"; Céline; Pauline (in the Carmel); Léonie; Félicité (household servant); the Guérins.
- Source: LT 21 (to Marie, 2 Oct 1886), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-21-a-marie-2-octobre-1886/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Tous les jours la Ste Vierge a eu un cierge et je l'ai tant priée et suppliée [...] Je t'en conjure ne me rapporte rien cela me fera vraiment de la peine. [...] Félicité te dit bien des choses elle est d'une humeur charmante depuis que tu est partie »
- Translation: "Every day the Blessed Virgin had a candle, and I prayed and begged her so much [...] I beg you, don't bring me anything back; it would really hurt me. [...] Félicité sends her regards; she has been in a charming mood since you left."
- New to English readers: yes — not in 1911 or baseline.
- Confidence: medium — the facts of the letter are clear, but who "le Père" is and the purpose of the trip are not stated.
- Notes: The letter does not say who "le Père" is (possibly Fr Pichon himself, but "Monsieur Pichon" writes separately, so it may be a different person). Marie entered the Carmel on 15 October 1886 (baseline); this letter is two weeks before. The line about Félicité's mood since Marie left may be a joke about Marie's strictness; the letter does not explain it.
